And that is why there is an emergency complaint so that those Poles who have not yet been able to challenge the ruling, which all the lawyers to whom they have addressed, tell them that it is clearly illegal, only there is no legal remedy to challenge it, to say that there is such a measure. [[Adjournment from 10 a.m. 15 min. 29 hours 15 min 33]] I'm reopening the session. We proceed with the examination of item 25 of the agenda: First reading of the government draft law amending certain laws in connection with the shortening of the period of storage of employee files and their electronicisation (print No 1995). I ask the Undersecretary of State at the Ministry of Development of Mr Mariusz Haladyj to present the justification for the project.
ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-08-sejm-53-3.u683	Mrs. Marshal! Ladies and gentlemen! In Poland today, employers are obliged to keep employee records for a period of 50 years from the end of their employment relationship, and only in paper form. Such a long, 50-year period of record keeping is a European phenomenon, e.g. in Denmark it is 5 years, in Finland or Germany - 10. The duration of this obligation, on the one hand, entails, of course, the burden and costs for employers, but on the other hand, the employees themselves do not guarantee that, when applying to the Social Security Office for a pension, they will be able to receive a pension of the amount due. The main reason for such a long 50-year period of retention of employee records is the need to submit together with an application for pension or pension proof of periods of employment and the amount of remuneration received. As a result, it is the worker's responsibility today to prove all the information necessary for the correct calculation of the pension or pension. The employee who makes an application requests, in particular, often after many years, the former employer to issue an appropriate certificate for the Social Security Office. Hence the employer's obligation to keep this documentation. However, it may not be possible for an employee to collect all the documents he needs because he cannot determine where they are stored. The reason may be that the employer has ceased his activity and either has not kept the relevant documentation or has given it to an unreliable storekeeper. As a result, the documentation was destroyed or lost. The lack of evidence in turn reduces the amount of the benefit. The protection of the employee turns out to be illusory in this situation, because despite the mechanisms that exist in theory to prevent the destruction of these documents... This mechanism in this situation simply does not work because it does not really respect the rules, but also because of the design of this provision. While it can be said that such an obligation had a greater justification in the economy of the previous system when we had several large state companies, conglomerates, which were known to store these documents almost as much as the office, the situation is completely different in a time of free economy, when employers are often natural persons engaged in business activities or small partnerships, most often public. This mechanism then ceases to work to the detriment of the workers themselves, because in today's economy we are dealing with more labour mobility between workplaces, but we also have more dynamic transformations of companies themselves, because companies are failing, being taken over, being liquidated, connecting, etc. Experience shows that employee records, in a little colloquial terms, do not keep pace with the natural fluctuations of economic operators in the free market economy. This security today is illusory because, theoretically, the employer is obliged to keep this documentation, but this employee for 20, 30, 40 years will have to prove how long he worked, what remuneration he received, etc. If he doesn't prove it, he'll get a minimum pension. But he won't know until he's applied for a pension or a pension. Of course, however to criticize employers in such a situation, it is also necessary to say objectively and honestly that employers do not have such an incentive to properly fulfil these obligations - de facto for the State, because social security in the form of pensions or pensions is a relationship between an employee and the State and not an employer, i.e. an example of regulation where the State unwarrantedly transfers its obligation to entrepreneurs. However, from this brief presentation of the problem, it is not enough just to shorten this period, because, of course, from the employer's point of view, this will solve the problem, while from the employee's point of view... He will be deprived of this protection, even if it is residual or illusory today. Eventually, he'd have to... [[There's water here.]] [[Here, Minister.]] Thank you very much. [[It's okay.]] I apologize for my indisposition. Alternatively, the staff member would have to collect and store the documentation himself. Another reason for storing employee records is the possibility of using it as evidence or in court proceedings, in matters of employment relationship, or in criminal, civil, administrative matters. This period is also important from the point of view of the proposed new regulations, because most of the claims on the employment relationship expire 3 years from the day the claim became due, or from the day on which the injured party learned of the damage. In such a case, however, that period may not exceed 10 years from the date on which the injurious event occurred. When we look at the Civil Code, we see that there are generally 10-year limitation periods. Hence, this 10-year period, which we are proposing, is about to guarantee that employers themselves can also make effective claims. What are the costs of 50-year documentation storage? We estimate that these costs for the economy amount to about 130 million PLN per year, i.e. - moving this to micro level - for a company that employs several hundred employees, they can reach several hundred thousand PLN. These are of course costs of work, paper, printing, transport, rented surfaces, specialized equipment, e.g. closets. In fact, we are talking here not only about private employers, but also about public and local employers. As I mentioned, today these files can only be kept and kept on paper. Employers creating them in electronic form can only treat such a version as an auxiliary version. Enabling electronic documentation storage will, you can say, adapt it to the technologies available today or other activities carried out by the employer, which are already undergoing newer technologies. As a result of cooperation between the Ministry of Development, the Ministry of Family, Labour and Social Policy, the Department of Social Insurance and the Ministry of Digitalization, we have developed solutions that are included in the presented project. On the one hand, he is, of course, implementing - which is the first obvious result - the demands of entrepreneurs to reduce administrative obligations, but on the other hand, if we look at it, we see that this project also improves the situation of workers, because it gives greater security to data on future pensions. This project consists of three parts. Firstly, it reduces the retention time of the employer's employee file from 50 to 10 years, but with the simultaneous transfer of data to the Social Insurance Institution. Secondly, it allows employers to store the documentation electronically. Thirdly, it indicates the non-cash form of payment of remuneration as default. The first group of changes is certainly the most serious and the largest, i.e. shortening the retention period of employee files. It will apply to all employment relationships which will start after the date of entry into force of the Act, the employment of the employer concerned and not the employment of the person concerned at all. In other words, if we were to maintain the proposed solution, we would have a solution in force from 1 January 2019 and in relation to the labour relations that have been concluded since then. Such a significant reduction in this period is possible by means of the fact that the employer will be obliged to provide the Social Security Office with the data necessary for determining and granting future pensions in the current monthly reports. This will of course require the creation of an IT system in the ZUS, hence the extended vacathio legis on 1 January 2019. As a result, the insured person will not have to submit additional evidence when applying for a pension, nor will he have to look for it from his employer or from a company that stores these documents, since the Social Security Office will receive them from the employer on an ongoing basis, and will therefore have them. In view of the fact that the employer will send data to the ZUS, employees from active participants of the process will become persons verifying the correctness of the data collected in the ZUS and will first of all have permanent access to copies of the employees' documentation from the employer and their individual accounts in the ZUS. This will allow them to keep up to date with their data and, if necessary, request an adjustment or an investigation of the Social Security Office. Each employee employed after the entry into force of the Act, once he has completed his work, will be informed with a work certificate of how much time his records will be kept, and of the fact that he may at any time obtain a copy of the documents and that, after the period indicated, he will be able to collect all his documentation from the employer. Given that the transfer of data to the Social Security Office creates a situation that we believe is beneficial for both employees and employers, we have also tried to ensure that this scope of application of the law can cover earlier periods of employment. Thus, the employer will be able to move to a 10-year period of retention of employee records for employees employed after 31 December 1998 and before the date of entry into force of this law, but only if he makes an appropriate statement to the Social Security Office and provides an information report. Such information report shall be submitted to the Social Security Office and shall contain the data necessary for the calculation of the employee's pension related to the employment relationship concerned. This will be primarily the data on paid income necessary to establish the basis for the assessment of the benefit, and data that are specific to certain occupational groups to which supplementary pension benefits have been granted, such as. like teachers or railroad workers. This information, as in the case of persons whose data are transmitted on an ongoing basis, will be deposited in an individual insured account. Data from employee records should be safe in the Social Security Office, as the Social Security Office is prepared to store data, already collects data related to pensions and therefore for most of the current employees the scope of the information transmitted to the Social Security Office will increase slightly. What we often hear about is problems with getting a pension. These problems are related to the inability to obtain information... not so much the inability to obtain information from the Social Security Office, but just the problems of obtaining documents from employers who either no longer exist, either passed the documentation to an unreliable storekeeper or lost it. This 50-year period is long. The transfer of data to the CSO therefore eliminates the problem of keeping records and relying only on the employer, i.e. this country in this case will take over the role, the obligation to store these data, which will then be needed to calculate the pension. This will be used to build an IT system in the Social Insurance Institution. What is also important, worth emphasising, is that the employer's decision will depend on use this option for employees between 1999 and the date of entry into force of the Act. If this doesn't go away, it's gonna be 50 years. If the employer decides to submit reports and shorten the storage period, he will have to do so for all persons employed in this time period. What we've been thinking about working on this project is creating a system for informing employees and former employees about changing the storage period. Therefore, the employer will have to issue a copy of this report to the employee for whom he will submit an information report and inform the employee of the period of storage of his file, the right to receive it and the right to obtain a copy. The obligation to inform employees of the report will also rest with the Social Security Office. In the annual statement of the account, the employee will receive the same instruction as the employer is required to provide it. The added value of the current situation is that the change concerns the collection of data by the Social Security Office not only of employees but also of contractors. Also in this case, ZUS will receive information and data about such persons. Every insured person will have access to the data collected in the account via the ZUS Electronic Services Platform. The insured will therefore be able to control the amount of contributions transferred and other information to be shown in the PUE as a result of the changes. If the insured person finds that all the contributions have not been entered in his account or that they have been written in the wrong amount, first, he will be able to ask the employer to correct the data and, if this does not work, to conduct an investigation by the Social Security Office. On the other hand, the retention period for persons employed before 1999 will remain 50 years old, as it is today, due to a different way of transmitting - until then, i.e. until the reform of the ZUS - data to the plant. There's no shortcut here. The short explanation is that before 1999, the employer, or payer, only provided the Social Security Office with information on the amount of contributions paid for all employees, without being able to assign them to the insured person, without assigning them to the insured person the appropriate amount of the contributions. This means that the Social Security Office cannot independently determine the amount of these contributions or verify these data. The second part of the project concerns the possibility of electronicisation of documentation and its storage in the same form, with these changes with a shorter period being independent of each other. Electronicisation will be possible in relation to all documents, and those that already exist, and those that will arise in the future, here there are no limits. Electronics, of course, will not be an obligation. Now, of course, the employer can make electronic copies. Today they are merely ancillary - not evidence, not decisive. What results from the digitisation of employee records is the acceleration of the process of drafting the documents themselves, shortening the time of searching the files, improving the quality of the documentation, because there is no wearing process, and reducing the cost of storing them, as well as easier access to them. The key, of course, in this context will be to ensure that solutions are safe for those using them. Therefore, this issue was discussed particularly with the Ministry of Digitalization, but also with experts in the field of electronic documentation security. The possibility of keeping and storing employee records in electronic form requires, first, clarification of the conditions, and they are presented in the draft law, but also a regulation will be issued in which very detailed conditions will already be laid down on how to ensure the durability, completeness and confidentiality of the documentation. With regard to electronic documentation, it will also be possible to switch from paper to electronic as well as electronic to paper. The employer will be obliged to inform the employees of any change. Proof of employee documentation will be equivalent to paper documentation. And the employee, and the employer will be able to use paper and electronic documents to the same extent. However, as I said, the specific issues will be in the regulation. The employee shall be able to receive free copies, in accordance with the provision, throughout their storage period and, importantly, in any form, i.e. not only in the electronic version, because it can also request the printing and authentication of such documents. The last amendment concerns the introduction of a form of non-cash payment of remuneration as a preferred or default form, while maintaining the possibility of payment of remuneration in cash at the request of the employee. Currently, as we know, the rule provides that the basic principle is to pay the employee's wages. Another way is acceptable if we have a collective agreement or if the employee agrees to it in writing, the change here is actually a kind of adaptation to today's employee preferences, because the NBP data shows that 87% employees receive payment to account - 87% having a personal account. Of course, it reduces unnecessary bureaucracy, since most workers today benefit from it, but also remember that the non-cash form has a better impact on reducing the grey area. To sum up, first of all, this is obviously a project that meets the expectations of anti-Bureaucratic entrepreneurs themselves. This is a postulate that has been made for years, that is what they have proposed. This will allow for more modern management of employee documentation, which gives us digitization. But also the changes that were originally intended to relieve the burden on entrepreneurs show that this draft legal act also increases the safety of workers and their future rights to pensions, because this obligation, which today is incumbent on private employers and possibly ceded to a company that stores these documents, does not work in practice, does not give safety and guarantees to the employee. Therefore, it is the obligation for the employer to transfer the documentation to the Social Security Office in order to shorten the period of retention of the documentation. Therefore, I think this is a project that benefits both the one side and the other side of the employment relationship. Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! The Council of Ministers requests that the Act on the amendment of certain laws be submitted in connection with the shortening of the period of storage of employee files and their electronicisation for further parliamentary work. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-08-sejm-53-3.u687	Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! On behalf of the Parliamentary Club, the Civic Platform has the honour to present its position on the government draft law amending certain laws in connection with the shortening of the period of storage and electronicisation of employee files. The aim of the bill is to introduce four key legal solutions. Firstly, this will reduce the period of mandatory retention of employee records from 50 to 10 years. And you'd like to say, oh, finally. Secondly, the provisions contained in the government draft law allow employers to keep and store employee records in electronic form. Every employer, but also every employee in the HR department, knows what a nightmare documents are kept in the paper version, because so far there has been such a requirement, and everyone also knows that the current computer programs used by entrepreneurs already allow them to be archived. Only the relevant legal provisions, which are included in this draft law, were missing. Thirdly, the law introduces the principle of priority for payment of remuneration to a bank account, as has already been discussed here twice in a very large way. Luckily, the choice was left that if the employee wanted to receive a wage in his hands, i.e. in the so-called. cash, this can be done at his request to his employer. Fourthly, the procedated provisions contained in the government draft law create a legal framework that will enable employees to receive employee documents not only after the end of their working life but also after the end of their storage period. The law we are discussing contains solutions expected by employers and employees in Poland. These provisions aim to facilitate the functioning of both groups. They also introduce European standards in the Polish legal system for storing employee records. They also clarify the existing rules governing this area of business and workers. To some extent, the bill follows the recommendations of the Supreme Audit Chamber in the September 2017 report on the problems of keeping documentation for the calculation of pension benefits. In the NIK's view, employees of work facilities wound up before 1 January 1999 often have major problems with establishing the entity keeping the necessary documentation. The NI shall also record cases where unauthorised entities keep records. Both reasons make the Social Insurance Institution, based on applicable law, calculate pension benefits at a lower level than a person would be entitled to if archiving were easier and documents were in the Social Security Office. This draft law contains provisions that allow companies to reduce the retention period of employee records from 50 years to 10 years. Why is that crucial? Please note that an employer who transfers personal files in accordance with applicable regulations to relevant public archives and stores them for a period of 50 years must pay for each year of archiving. Therefore, reducing this period from 50 years to 10 years will certainly make it easier for employers and it is an element that will save money, but above all it is a step forward, because if we have the ability to store documents in electronic form, then there will be no need to submit them to the archives, while the costs of this storage will be much, much lower. A number of legal solutions have been proposed. It is commendable that the government has been working on this bill for over a year. The bill was addressed to a public consultation of up to 110 entities. Only 17 of them submitted comments, while most of the comments submitted were taken into account in the draft law. Therefore, I believe that formal requirements for correct legislation have been met. Some records may raise doubts, but I hope... This applies, for example, to the statement of will expressed in electronic form, the principle of inheritance, e.g. who will first have the right to collect documents after a deceased employee, whether the wife or daughter will have priority, or whether the rule in the Civil Code will apply. Such questions arise, but in my opinion the project is very good and is moving in a very good direction. I hope that we will have the opportunity to clarify all these issues during the committee work. And that is also the recommendation of the Civic Platform club. Thank you. ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-08-sejm-53-3.u713	Dear Marshal! High Izbo! Ladies and gentlemen! In the first words, I would like to express my sincere thanks for the opportunity to present a report on compliance with patient rights in the territory of the Republic of Poland, covering the years 2015 and 2016, although I did not act as ombudsman at the time. Prime Minister Beata Szydło called me to this honorable and very responsible position on 30 October this year. I would like to point out very briefly that I would like to significantly strengthen the position of the Ombudsman and his role in the health system in the initial term of office of the ombudsman. I will want to achieve this, among others. by amending the concept of cooperation with public authorities, including in particular the Minister for Health. Nor would this be possible without increasing the effectiveness of cooperation with non-governmental organisations active in the area of patient rights. During the first period of activity, I would also like to focus on the health reforms currently being implemented. I will always support solutions that benefit patients. Turning to the assessment of the degree of compliance with patient rights in Poland in 2015 and 2016, I would like to stress at the same time that this assessment concerns historical events, while at the moment there are already systemic changes in the area of health policy, changes for the better, which I will signal at the end of the speech. The rights of the patient shall be reflected in the constitutional right to health contained in Article. 68 The Constitution of the Republic of Poland, and its purpose is to provide the patient with maximum safety and comfort in the treatment process. These rights are granted to each patient, regardless of whether the treatment takes place under public funds or in a commercial system. At the same time, I am aware that the conclusions of the reports are difficult to accept. Namely, compliance with the patient's rights in the territory of the Republic of Poland between 2015 and 2016 was unsatisfactory, in particular in 2015, when 71 000 were received by the ombudsman. various types of notifications, including signals, cases and queries. This has been the highest value since the appointment of the institution of the ombudsman. In the Ombudsman's view, this shows on the one hand that citizens are constantly aware of the rights of the patient and on the other hand that they are not satisfied with respect for those rights, which is, of course, part of the assessment presented. The main factors influencing the assessment of compliance with the patient's rights have been constant for years, the degree of funding for health services, although, as I would like to draw your attention to, limited financial resources are not only a domain of the health system and cannot be justified by the insufficient level of compliance with the patient's rights, which is also affected by non-financial factors. To include among others: the management, organisation of healthcare provision in medical entities, the approach of healthcare professionals to the patient and the failure of healthcare professionals to comply with the law. At the same time, it must be borne in mind that it is simply impossible to eliminate the risks associated with the provision of health services completely. Together, however, we should strive to maximise the adverse consequences of medical interventions. The report sets out the degree of compliance with the individual patient's rights for both 2015 and 2016. In 2015, the patient's right to medical records and patients' right to health services were assessed as not highly respected. The Ombudsman took a number of actions within the framework of his competences to improve compliance with the above mentioned. patients' rights, whose effectiveness can be assessed over a period of several years. Initiatives focused in particular on access to health services, the problem of underfinancing these benefits, insufficient medical doctors, nurses and midwives, and the need to develop standards in certain areas of medicine. In 2015, we observed an increase in respect of patient's rights to health information and consent to the provision of health services, which the Ombudsman stressed in the summary and recommendations of the 2014 Patient Rights Report. The main problem, as in previous years, was too long a waiting period for health services. This was influenced, among others. insufficient funding for healthcare services and insufficient number of specialist doctors. The assessment of compliance with patient rights in the territory of the Republic of Poland in 2016 was also based on an analysis of identified violations of patient rights, supplemented by identified areas of risk of compliance with these rights and the actions of the ombudsman in this respect. Before presenting the 2016 evaluation, I would like to draw your attention to the positive conclusions of the analysis of patient-led notifications or signals in 2016. Patients decided to consider a positive change in the health system, among others. introduction of free medicines for seniors, so-called programme 75+. The Ombudsman received many signals supporting this solution and expressing his full approval. The second noticeable change during this period is changes in oncology treatment. Since the entry into force of the oncology package, the number of signals on access to oncology benefits has been falling systematically. Finally, 2016 was a period of increased discussion on health reform. In order to present an assessment of compliance with patient rights in 2016, it should be considered that these rights have not been respected satisfactorily. This mainly applies, as in 2015, to patients' rights to health services and medical records, which were already the most frequently violated for the next year. With regard to the first of these rights, attention should be paid to insufficient access to specific health services, which is obviously influenced by a number of factors. With regard to patient's right to medical records, the main concerns raised the legal conduct of healthcare providers. The year 2016 was the period of intensive work of the ombudsman as the competent authority for the protection of the patient's rights. The Ombudsman's office was visited by 69,000. various types of applications. The Ombudsman has conducted investigations into individual cases and cases concerning practices that violate the patient's collective rights. As regards this second point, it is noteworthy that in 2016 13 decisions by the ombudsman were brought before the administrative courts, of all 51 issued in the reporting year, but no decision was validly challenged by the administrative courts, which demonstrates their regularity and legality. The Ombudsman also carried out other statutory tasks aimed at directly influencing the health system. These include cooperation with non-governmental organisations as part of the citizens' health project. There were also optimistic accents. In 2016, for the first time, the number of confirmed violations of the patient's rights decreased by 9 percentage points, indicating an improvement in compliance with the patient's rights compared to the previous year. In particular, compliance with patient's rights to information and consent to the provision of health services has been improved. With regard to administrative proceedings, it is worth mentioning the most important successes of the ombudsman in this area, in the area of judicial proceedings. These are among others. favourable decisions of voivodship administrative courts and the Supreme Administrative Court concerning, inter alia, the lack of the right of medicinal products to charge for the provision of copies of medical records or the access of patients to original medical records. During the period covered by the report, the Ombudsman stepped up activities of a systemic nature in the area of broadly understood health protection. In 2015, the Ombudsman took 438 initiatives of this type. They were the result of a systematic analysis of the information obtained through investigations, investigations into practices affecting the collective rights of patients or information provided by patients through the helpline. During the period covered by the report, the Ombudsman made active use of the possibility of requesting or taking part in civil proceedings already pending concerning the violation of patient rights under the rights of the prosecutor. To sum up the issue of patient rights, it should be stressed that the human resources of the healthcare provider, its equipment, organisation and working conditions, as well as the financial situation have a fundamental impact on the patient's care process. There seems to be no turning back from a policy of improving health care. All stakeholders involved in the organisation of health care services should depend on the development of the optimal health care model in Poland as soon as possible. Finally, I would like to point out what I had already signaled at the beginning of the speech that these reports relate to the years 2015 and 2016. Today, however, there are many positive systemic changes that are taking place in the broad range of health services. Very positive signals on systemic changes, including legislative ones, reach the spokesperson on the part of the patient organisations, which as the voice of the patients themselves are the most meaningful reflection of the opinions of the patients themselves. These changes should be mentioned among others. planned gradual increase in health expenditure up to 6% gross domestic product and extensive consultation of systemic changes with patient organisations and taking into account in the course of the legislative work the comments of the social side, which is extremely important in the assessment of the ombudsman. Mrs. Marshal! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, Thank you kindly for your attention. [[Applause]]

The information provided by the ombudsman shows that compliance with patients' rights was highly unsatisfactory in 2015. In 2016, the patient's rights were also added 100%. , i.e. those for which the patient's rights spokesperson has not found a single infringement throughout the year. This is the right of the patient to report adverse effects of medicinal products and the right of the patient to pastoral care. In 2016, the number of confirmed violations of the patient's law decreased by 9 percentage points, i.e. improving compliance with patient rights compared to 2015. The greatest improvement is seen when the patient's rights to information and consent to the provision of health services are respected. By 8 percentage points - from 24% in 2015 to 16% in 2016 - the percentage of infringements found in relation to all identified breaches of the patient's rights decreased. In the evaluation of 2016, consideration should be given to the positive findings of the analysis of patient notifications and signals. Patients consider it a positive change in the health system, among others. 75+ programme, i.e. introduction of free medicines for seniors. Since the entry into force of the oncology package, there has also been a decrease in the number of signals that concern access to oncology benefits, especially diagnostics. It should be stressed that 2016 was a period of intensive work in the field of health reform, in particular the creation of a new model of basic health care or the basic system of hospital care provision - a network of hospitals. The introduction of changes in this area will certainly have a significant impact on the implementation of patients' rights and allow them to look forward with hope. Parliamentary Club Law and Justice will vote in favour of both reports. Thank you very much. [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-08-sejm-53-3.u720	Thank you very much, Member. Please, Mr Jerzy Kozłowski, Kukiz Club15.
ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-08-sejm-53-3.u721	Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! On behalf of the Kukiz Club, I have the honour to present our position on the 2015-2016 reports on compliance with patient rights in the territory of the Republic of Poland. Constitution of the Republic of Poland, Art. 68: Everyone has the right to health protection. Citizens, regardless of their material situation, have equal access to public-funded healthcare services by public authorities. In reality, it is possible to talk about the responsibilities of the patient in the Polish health system. Every citizen working or retiring is obliged to pay a health contribution, and not one, because in the case of workers - from any employment relationship, contract or contract for work. A citizen engaged in an economic activity also has a duty to pay contributions from each of them. Health contribution in the remuneration of, for example, 3 000. net is PLN 325 per month. The citizen, wanting not to, becomes a patient in time. And here comes the next responsibilities, e.g. duty to wait in queues. If someone does not believe what these queues look like in Poland, I can present here an example of voters from my constituency, who received a referral to the hospital in Ustroń for 2033 with a beautiful note that if he pays, he will be able to arrive in the next 14 days, which is to reduce this time when financing out of his own pocket is practically 16 years old. A similar situation is with other expectations and it is not true - because I consulted it before coming to the Sejm - that the queues were shortened when it comes to cataract surgery. I've consulted with several medical facilities of this kind. It was denied, it was said that the queues did not shorten, and it was feared that from 1 January they would be even longer. That's the reality. There's a list in front of me, black and white, but there's, uh, a source: the OECD, on a comparison of several medical treatments in relation to other countries. This is where cataracts are indicated - 441 days. Compared to Estonia - 253 days - and Denmark - 87 days - we are a record holder. The same is true of hip, knee, or other medical procedures. This is the real responsibility of the patient: the obligation to wait in line. What other responsibilities does the patient have in Poland? It has e.g. the obligation to bear the costs of diagnostic tests, because first contact doctors, because there is no separate budget for diagnostic tests, are very reluctant to give such referrals. I don't have to look far for examples. My wife has this problem. He needs to be tested frequently, and once a year he gets assigned to the diagnostics, and he pays for the others out of his own pocket. It's a good thing she got it because she can afford it, but there are a lot of people in Poland who can't afford it. Where's the prevention? Where is the early detection of disease? It's not there. We are supposed to talk about patient rights, but we can mention the basics: the right to health care, the right to health services in accordance with the requirements of current medical knowledge, the right to health services carried out with due care, the right to immediate provision of health services due to health or life risks, etc. The Ombudsman pointed out that, as in previous years and 2015 and 2016 we had a problem with access to health services. There are problems with access to medical records, compliance with the right to information, and problems with the protection of patient's right to respect for intimacy and dignity. And now I'm going to present you with a personal matter. In 2015, in March, I was well trained in how the Polish health system works. As a man who hadn't been sick until then, except for one chronic, difficult disease, or diabetes, I went to... I mean, I was sent by a doctor of first contact to the hospital for immediate surgery without being a patient of a private surgery chief's office in my beloved city. Because I wasn't a patient of this chief, despite being assigned to an immediate operation, because there was a very serious life-threatening situation, and that has to be said straight away, the surgery was postponed to force me to do something. I'm not gonna talk in this room about what, anyone can guess. And if it wasn't for the help of the nurses who were so directing me, that despite the administration of glucose to me without consulting a doctor who deals with diabetes... My sugar was raised at 480. If it wasn't for those nurses who risked losing their jobs, cheated on their jobs because they gave me things that... They didn't give the things the chief suggested. If it weren't for the doctor, a great surgeon who at his risk made a stand-by decision about immediate surgery in the absence of the chief, I would be dead on Monday. These are facts. This is the right of the patient in Poland. People like me -- because I've talked to a lot of patients in this ward -- there's a lot of people. There are those who don't go through Mr. Chief's private office. There are certainly few such chiefs in Poland, I hope, but they are. And as long as it is possible to combine this, unfortunately - it is such a phenomenon that the doctor, who is the head of the department, uses his department, for which we pay from public funds, for the purposes of private customer service or patients in his office - the patient's rights will be horribly, terribly raped. Now, after this personal little memory of mine, which is in my memory so far, and I probably will never erase myself from this memory, I would like to refer to the very development of your spokesperson, to whom we must address with due respect, because this is a good job - the good work of the Ombudsman - because patients feel that it is finally possible to report to him. It's just that everything we read here only applies to those who came forward. And how many people don't just report to the spokesperson? How many cases are missing because someone doesn't want to just or doesn't want to go through these difficult procedures? We really need to address a few issues here. I have already told you what the right of the patient to health services looks like in Poland, and I will not continue to talk about it. Now for the next point, feeding patients in hospitals. There's nothing to hide, there's a daily financial right, it's about feeding a patient, it's much less than a man who's in prison. We know what those hospital meals look like. It's an underfunding that's just embarrassing right now. And now, the number of doctors. There's drama with doctors, just like there's drama with nurses. There's a shortage of specialist doctors. This is not, as I said before, the issue of 2-year negligence, it is the negligence of many years. We feel, as patients, we have an incredible difficulty getting to a specialist's doctor. I am still from the 100 thousandth city that has these specialists; if not, I will find a specialist here, in Wielkopolska, in the big city of Poznan, I also have near to Łódź, but how many people who live far from such centres as they have difficulty? I'd like to focus on the nurses right now. Not long ago, I was invited in Wielkopolska, in Jarocin, to meet with nurses who told me something I knew earlier from a meeting in Kalisz. If it weren't for nurses working in a working system at their workplace, i.e. in their hospital, and going to other hospitals for contracts, I would say with my hand at heart that if they were only working on a single job in the Polish system - there would be no nurses to care for the sick from day to day. And I think you all know that. Right now, at the hospital in Jarocin, they're trying to negotiate with the president, because that's where the president's in charge, by the way. I don't have to explain to you that in the whole health care system payments are much lower than what should be. We Polish patients want to have the right to treat rested doctors, well-paid nurses. And what rest can we talk about in the case of doctors who are now pronouncing an opt-out clause, so they normally want to work, like a normal doctor, 48 hours? They are being harassed by the minister, called, defined in a manner simply derogatory of the doctor's dignity. You can't discredit people for wanting to work in decent conditions, in accordance with health care rules, and so that they don't... and there have been cases, I don't want to mention them very much that doctors were dying on duty. That's why we patients want a system that's friendly to us and we're waiting for a law that's not about health, it's about patient law. Thank you.
